Delay-driven dynamics in a host–vector model of canine Chagas disease
Authors: Ali Raza, Umar Shafique, Marek Lampart, Mansoor Alsulami, Emad Fadhal
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
The study introduces a mathematical model for the transmission dynamics of canine Chagas disease caused by Trypanosoma cruzi within a host–vector framework. The interaction between triatomine vectors and peridomestic dogs is described by a deterministic delay differential equation model incorporating biologically relevant time delays associated with vector development and infection processes. The vector population is structured into developmental stages, while the adult population is further stratified based on feeding behavior to reflect epidemiological relevance. The qualitative properties of the model are rigorously analyzed, including positivity, boundedness, and existence of solutions, ensuring biological feasibility. Equilibrium points are derived, and the basic reproduction number is computed using the next-generation matrix method to characterize the threshold dynamics of disease transmission. A sensitivity analysis of the reproduction number is also performed to identify key epidemiological parameters influencing disease spread. First-order and second-order nonstandard finite difference (NSFD) schemes are developed to preserve essential qualitative properties such as stability, positivity, and boundedness of the continuous model. Numerical simulations are conducted to validate the analytical findings and to investigate the influence of delay parameters, transmission rates, and discretization effects on the system dynamics. The results indicate that delay mechanisms play a significant role in reducing effective transmission and altering transient dynamics, while transmission parameters strongly influence disease persistence and spread. Overall, the proposed framework provides useful insights into the complex dynamics of canine Chagas disease and offers a reliable tool for exploring control strategies and long-term epidemiological behavior.

Introduction

Compartmental vector host transmission models describing the spread of T. cruzi between triatomine vectors and canine hosts were formulated to incorporate the influences of seasonal variability and vector migration on epidemiological dynamics. Subsequently, a non-seasonal framework, a seasonally modulated system, and a spatially coupled configuration were examined to assess the efficacy of fluralaner-based insecticidal interventions in diverse treatment-duration scenarios and transmission environments1. A compartmental epidemiological framework was formulated to represent the transmission dynamics of Trypanosoma cruzi among dogs, humans, cats, rodents, and triatomine vectors. The model further evaluated the epidemiological influence of all or nothing and leaky vaccination mechanisms, while parameter sensitivity was examined by univariate sensitivity analysis2. Mathematical models exploring the role of dogs in Chagas disease transmission were critically examined, evaluating how these models advanced the understanding of canine-mediated spread and control, and highlighting their methodological strengths and limitations3. The equilibrium states of a mathematical model that describes the dynamics of the disease were analyzed to understand the long-term behavior of the system4. Based on the Ross MacDonald model, a mechanistic model was developed to explain the response of transmission of T. cruci to changes in temperature. Life-history data from triatomine vectors were used to infer temperature-dependent parameters through Bayesian methods, which were then applied to generate a thermal suitability index, S(T), reflecting the potential transmission risk5. A dual-patch framework was constructed to investigate the interplay between virulent and non-virulent T. cruzi strains, evaluating the impact of strain migration on Chagas disease emergence in the southern United States. Dynamical analysis identified six potential outcomes, in which competitive exclusion prevailed in isolation, while migration promoted coexistence and alternative epidemiological patterns6. A novel mathematical model was formulated to capture the domestic transmission dynamics of Chagas disease, a parasitic infection that affects humans and various mammals in Central and South America7. An ordinary differential equation framework was devised to integrate innate and adaptive immune mechanisms for the study of T. cruzi infection, incorporating cardiomyocytes to mechanistically represent the effects of the pathogen in cardiac tissue8. Two independently constructed models, the PHICOR/CIDMA and Princeton frameworks, were employed to simulate domestic transmission of T. cruzi, with their outputs compared to Venezuelan seroprevalence data from 1968–1998. The performance of the model was evaluated over the last 9 and 19 years of the data set, highlighting their comparative ability to replicate historical infection trends in different temporal windows9. An extensive review on Chagas disease was done in dogs with emphasis on its diagnosis, the eco-epidemiology, and dynamics of transmission and control of the disease and the use of dogs as a risk factor of human infection as well as the sentinel indicators of the disease presence in the environment took place in dogs10.

An alternative mathematical model was developed to model the domestic transmission processes through a system of four nonlinear differential equations that are interconnected. The method is useful in the study of the host-vector interactions in homes environments11. The spatial-temporal distribution of the disease in human populations was explained by the creation of a new mathematical model based on the partial differential reaction-diffusion equations and was used to explain the growth of the disease over time and space12. The systematic literature search was performed in PubMed, Scopus, and MESH using such keywords as dog, Trypanosoma cruzi, treatment (chemotherapy evaluation of T. cruzi), Leishmania chagasi, Leishmania infantum, canine visceral leishmaniasis, and treatment (chemotherapy assessment of Leishmania)13. Mathematical model was created to explain the dynamics of the Chagas disease in neighboring rural villages of the Great Chaco region and incorporated human movement, dispersal of vectors passively, and resistance to insecticides. Simulations were done in different scenarios to discuss possible epidemiological outcomes using computer simulations as a tool to achieve this goal14. Simulations were carried out in different scenarios with the aim of discussing possible epidemiological outcomes using computer simulations as a tool to reach this goal. The simulations were conducted in different scenarios to explore possible epidemiological outcomes15. Based on the earlier models of vector-borne and congenital transmission, a comprehensive model was created, combining both of the Chagas disease transmission pathways, and the simulations were performed to study the joint dynamics of the two aspects of transmission15. It created a new mathematical model to investigate the dynamics of Chagas disease transmission, of which humans and domestic animals are infected in Central America and South America rural regions of the country16. The literature review included 1968 to 2022 and was dedicated to the epidemiology, clinical presentation, diagnosis, treatment, and prevention of Chagas disease in dogs and cats17. To explore the dynamics of Chagas disease, a stochastic model was developed to include a certain degree of randomness in the parameters of the model, and simulation was conducted to understand the effects of this disease on the behavior of the disease18.. The review also emphasized the importance of dogs as the main human reservoirs of T. cruzi and sentinel indicators of the disease, as it is highly susceptible and containing high levels of parasitemia, which made it easy to transmit to vectors19. The importance of dogs as sentinel indicators and the primary human reservoirs of T. cruzi was also noted, since it is highly susceptible and parasite-rich, making it easy to transmit to vectors20. The high levels of parasitemia was also observed as the reason why the disease is important as The difference in the efficacy and safety in benznidazole (BNZ) chemotherapy of Chagas disease was explained by the pharmacokinetic factors. The aim of the study was to determine the effect of experimental acute and chronic infections with the Berenice-78 strain of Trypanosoma cruzi on the BNZ pharmacokinetics in dogs20. There have been more recent developments on the use of delay differential equations and computational techniques in the study of infectious diseases. Specifically, recent developments in microbial risk assessment modeling21 and environmental system modeling22 show the significance of considering complex biological phenomena in mathematical modeling. Moreover, modeling studies that apply computational approaches in the context of combining epidemiological modeling with data-driven techniques have been developed in23. These studies highlight the increasing need to consider realistic biological phenomena, particularly delays, which prompted the formulation of the current model for canine Chagas disease. Although the canine Chagas disease has developed methods to control the disease, the classical fluralaner therapy models have mostly presumed an immediate effect ignoring the time delays in disease development, host-parasite interactions, and treatment. Delay modeling provides an effective modeling method in order to be able to represent such inherent time-dependent processes, in order to better model the dynamics of a disease. This study builds on the current models by adding time delays to the transmission and treatment model so as to determine the timing, effectiveness, and best timing of fluralaner regimens to enable better control of canine Chagas disease in diverse epidemiological contexts. However, there are still some significant challenges regarding the study of modeling for Chagas diseases even though it is widely researched. For example, many researchers do not take into consideration the biological time delays that occur, or simply consider transmission without any time delays at all. There are very few works that involve both a good mathematical structure of delay and numerical method. The present research will be focused on using the concept of delay and NSFD schemes to create an appropriate model for canine Chagas disease.

Comparison with non-delay models

Traditional models used to study the dynamics of Chagas’ disease usually consider transmission and progression of the disease as instantaneous events. Although non-delay models offer valuable information regarding average dynamics, these models cannot incorporate time lags present within natural biological systems, including delays caused by vector development, parasite incubation, and interactions between vectors and hosts. In this regard, the delay-driven model presented here is able to address this problem directly since the inclusion of delays helps describe the system in a more realistic manner through consideration of the time taken by the transmission process. The inclusion of delay terms also leads to several interesting dynamic behaviors which cannot be obtained in the absence of delays.

Originality and contribution

This work builds on an innovative host-vector model incorporating delays associated with the development and infection process for canine Chagas disease, going beyond traditional models of instantaneous disease transmission.

In terms of methodological innovation, the paper makes use of a combination of qualitative analysis and NSFD modeling to ensure positivity and boundedness in the model formulation.

Taken together, this work demonstrates how delays influence dynamics in epidemiological studies. This paper is organized as follows. Section Introduction introduces the problem and highlights its epidemiological relevance. Section Model formulation presents the mathematical model, incorporating biological assumptions and time-delay effects within the host–vector framework.

Section Qualitative analysis of the model is devoted to the qualitative analysis of the model, where fundamental properties such as existence, positivity, and boundedness of solutions are established. In Sect. Model equilibria and basic reproduction number, the equilibrium points are derived, and the basic reproduction number is computed, together with a sensitivity analysis to identify key parameters influencing disease transmission.

Section Design of the NSFD Schemes develops the proposed first-order and second-order nonstandard finite difference (NSFD) schemes and analyzes their structure-preserving properties. Section Epidemiological data and global prevalence in dogs discusses relevant epidemiological information used to support the modeling framework.

Section Graphical simulation and discussion presents numerical simulations that validate the theoretical results and illustrate the impact of key parameters, particularly time delays, on the system dynamics. Finally, Sect. Conclusion summarizes the main findings and outlines directions for future research.

Model formulation

We develop a deterministic time-delay model to describe the transmission dynamics of Trypanosoma cruzi between triatomine vectors and peridomestic dogs, which act as the principal reservoir host. To maintain analytical tractability, seasonal variability in vector dynamics is neglected and all epidemiological and demographic parameters are assumed constant over time. The triatomine population is structured into three developmental stages: eggs (E), nymphs (Y), and adults. Adult triatomines are further classified based on their feeding behavior. A proportion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi$$\end{document} of newly matured adults feeds on dogs and is therefore epidemiologically relevant, while the remaining proportion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-\phi )$$\end{document} feeds exclusively on incompetent hosts and does not contribute to disease transmission. Eggs are produced by adult triatomines at a per-capita oviposition rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document} and hatch into nymphs after a developmental delay \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _e$$\end{document}, representing the egg incubation period. Density-dependent survival from egg to nymph is incorporated through an environmental carrying capacity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document}. Nymphs mature into adults after a developmental delay \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _y$$\end{document}, accounting for the molting process. Vertical transmission of T. cruzi is ignored, and nymphs are assumed incapable of acquiring infection due to their limited feeding behavior. The inclusion of discrete time delays is biologically motivated by key processes in the transmission of Trypanosoma cruzi. Vector development from eggs to adult stages occurs over finite periods, represented by delays \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _h$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _v$$\end{document}, which are essential for realistic population dynamics. Additionally, infection transmission involves latency: vectors require time to become infectious after feeding on infected hosts (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _h$$\end{document}), and hosts also experience delays before infection is established (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _v$$\end{document}). Incorporating these delays improves the model’s ability to capture the temporal dynamics of host–vector interactions more realistically than instantaneous models. Among adult triatomines feeding on dogs, susceptible individuals (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{b1}$$\end{document}) may acquire infection (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{b1}$$\end{document}) after biting infectious dogs at transmission rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _v$$\end{document}. Adult triatomines feeding on non-competent hosts remain susceptible (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{b2}$$\end{document}) throughout their lifespan. Natural mortality rates for eggs, nymphs, and adult triatomines are denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _e$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _y$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _b$$\end{document}, respectively (Fig. 1).Fig. 1Flowchart of CCD.

Flowchart of CCD.

The dog population is assumed to remain constant (Fig. 1), with recruitment balancing natural mortality at rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _d$$\end{document}. Dogs are classified as susceptible (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_h$$\end{document}) or infectious (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_h$$\end{document}). Model parameters and their biological interpretation.

The model is derived by tracking the movement of individuals through epidemiological and developmental compartments according to biologically defined transition mechanisms. Eggs are produced by the total adult vector population at per-capita rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document}, giving rise to the recruitment term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda N_b(t)$$\end{document} in the egg equation. Eggs leave the compartment either by hatching into nymphs at rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document} or by natural mortality at rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _e$$\end{document}, which yields the loss term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau +\delta _e)E(t)$$\end{document}. Newly hatched nymphs enter the immature vector class after accounting for environmental limitation through the logistic-type factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\frac{Y(t)+N_b(t)}{\kappa }$$\end{document}, while nymphs leave this class through maturation at rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _y$$\end{document} and death at rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _y$$\end{document}.

Among newly matured adults, a fraction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi$$\end{document} is assumed to feed on dogs and enters the susceptible epidemiologically active vector class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{b1}(t)$$\end{document}, whereas the remaining fraction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\phi$$\end{document} enters the non-transmitting class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{b2}(t)$$\end{document}. Infection in epidemiologically active vectors occurs when susceptible dog-feeding vectors contact infectious dogs. This transmission is represented by the bilinear incidence term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _v S_{b1}(t)\frac{I_h(t-\tau _h)}{N_h}e^{-\delta _b \tau _h}$$\end{document}, where the delayed host compartment reflects the time required for infection acquisition and establishment, and the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-\delta _b \tau _h}$$\end{document} accounts for survival of vectors during the delay period. Likewise, susceptible dogs become infected through contact with infectious dog-feeding vectors at rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _h S_h(t)\frac{I_{b1}(t-\tau _v)}{N_{b1}(t)}e^{-\delta _d \tau _v}$$\end{document}, where the delay \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _v$$\end{document} represents the lag associated with successful transmission and infection establishment in the host, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-\delta _d \tau _v}$$\end{document} represents host survival during that period. Based on the above biological assumptions and transition mechanisms, the transmission dynamics are formulated through the following system of delay differential equations:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dE(t)}{dt} = \lambda N_b(t) - \tau E(t) - \delta _e E(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dY(t)}{dt} = \tau E(t)\!\left( 1-\frac{Y(t)+N_b(t)}{\kappa }\right) - \gamma _y Y(t) - \delta _y Y(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dS_{b1}(t)}{dt} = \phi \gamma _y Y(t) - \beta _v S_{b1}(t)\frac{I_h(t-\tau _h)e^{-\delta _b \tau _h} }{N_h} - \delta _b S_{b1}(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dI_{b1}(t)}{dt} = \beta _v S_{b1}(t)\frac{I_h(t-\tau _h)e^{-\delta _b \tau _h}}{N_h} - \delta _b I_{b1}(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dS_{b2}(t)}{dt} = (1-\phi )\gamma _y Y(t) - \delta _b S_{b2}(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dS_h(t)}{dt} = \delta _d N_h (t) - \beta _h S_h(t)\frac{I_{b1}(t-\tau _v)e^{-\delta _d \tau _v}}{N_{b1}(t)} - \delta _d S_h(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dI_h(t)}{dt} = \beta _h S_h(t)\frac{I_{b1}(t-\tau _v)e^{-\delta _d \tau _v}}{N_{b1}(t)} - \delta _d I_h(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document} All the terms in the model (1)–(7) have an obvious interpretation. In Eq. (1), egg formation and losses because of hatching and mortalities are described. Equation (2) explains the process of developing nymphs with density dependence and deaths, while Eq. (3) explains the dynamics of the susceptible adult vectors together with recruitment and infection delay caused by infected dogs. In Eq. (4), infected vectors and their deaths are described, while Eq. (5) describes the vectors’ feeding behavior on non-competent hosts. Susceptible and infected dogs dynamics are explained in Eqs. (6) and (7). where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N_{b1}(t) = S_{b1}(t) + I_{b1}(t), \qquad N_b(t) = S_{b1}(t) + I_{b1}(t) + S_{b2}(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Since the proposed model incorporates multiple discrete time delays, the initial state of the system is prescribed through continuous and non-negative history functions defined on the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[-\tau _{\max },0]$$\end{document}, where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau _{\max }=\max \{\tau _h,\tau _v\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The initial conditions are given as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E(\theta ) = E_0(\theta ) \ge 0, \qquad Y(\theta ) = Y_0(\theta ) \ge 0, \qquad S_{b1}(\theta ) = S_{b1}^0(\theta ) \ge 0, \qquad I_{b1}(\theta )&= I_{b1}^0(\theta ) \ge 0, \\ S_{b2}(\theta ) = S_{b2}^0(\theta ) \ge 0, \qquad S_h(\theta ) = S_h^0(\theta ) \ge 0, \qquad I_h(\theta ) = I_h^0(\theta ) \ge 0, \qquad \end{aligned}$$\end{document}for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in [-\tau _{\max },0]$$\end{document}.

Qualitative analysis of the model

In this section, we establish the well-posedness of system (1)–(7), including existence, uniqueness, positivity, and boundedness of solutions.

Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N_{b1}(t)=S_{b1}(t)+I_{b1}(t), \qquad N_b(t)=S_{b1}(t)+I_{b1}(t)+S_{b2}(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N_h(t)=S_h(t)+I_h(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}We assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_h(t)>0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{b1}(t)>0$$\end{document} for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}, ensuring that all transmission terms are well-defined.

We define the biologically feasible region8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega =\left\{ (E,Y,S_{b1},I_{b1},S_{b2},S_h,I_h)\in {\mathbb {R}}_+^7: N_h(t)=N_h,\; 0\le Y(t)+N_b(t)\le \kappa ,\; E(t)\ge 0 \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Theorem 1

(Existence and uniqueness) Assume that the initial history functions are continuous and nonnegative on\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} [-\tau _{\max },0], \qquad \tau _{\max }=\max \{\tau _h,\tau _v\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Then system (1)–(7) admits a unique local solution for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}.

Moreover, if the solution remains bounded, then it extends uniquely to a global solution for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}.

Proof

The right-hand side of system (1)–(7) consists of continuous functions involving polynomial and rational terms together with discrete delays \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_h(t-\tau _h)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{b1}(t-\tau _v)$$\end{document}.

On the region where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_h(t)>0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{b1}(t)>0$$\end{document}, the system is locally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the state variables and delayed arguments. Therefore, by the standard theory of delay differential equations, a unique local solution exists.

The continuation of the solution follows from the same theory, and global existence is guaranteed once boundedness is established. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Lemma 1

The total host population satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N_h(t)=S_h(t)+I_h(t)=N_h \qquad \text {for all } t\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

Adding Eqs. (6) and (7), we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dN_h(t)}{dt} = \delta _dN_h(t)-\delta _dS_h(t)-\delta _dI_h(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_h(t)=S_h(t)+I_h(t)$$\end{document}, it follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dN_h(t)}{dt}=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_h(t)=N_h$$\end{document} for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Theorem 2

(Positivity of solutions) Assume that the initial history functions are continuous and nonnegative on\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} [-\tau _{\max },0]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Then all solutions of system (1)–(7) remain nonnegative for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}.

Proof

We verify that each state variable remains nonnegative.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(t)=0$$\end{document}, then from (1),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dE(t)}{dt}=\lambda N_b(t)\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y(t)=0$$\end{document}, then from (2),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dY(t)}{dt} = \tau E(t)\left( 1-\frac{Y(t)+N_b(t)}{\kappa }\right) \ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(t)\ge 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y(t)+N_b(t)\le \kappa$$\end{document}.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{b1}(t)=0$$\end{document}, then from (3),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dS_{b1}(t)}{dt}=\phi \gamma _yY(t)\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{b1}(t)=0$$\end{document}, then from (4),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dI_{b1}(t)}{dt} = \beta _v S_{b1}(t)\frac{I_h(t-\tau _h)e^{-\delta _b\tau _h}}{N_h}\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{b2}(t)=0$$\end{document}, then from (5),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dS_{b2}(t)}{dt}=(1-\phi )\gamma _yY(t)\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_h(t)=0$$\end{document}, then from (6),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dS_h(t)}{dt}=\delta _dN_h(t)\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_h(t)=0$$\end{document}, then from (7),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dI_h(t)}{dt} = \beta _h S_h(t)\frac{I_{b1}(t-\tau _v)e^{-\delta _d\tau _v}}{N_{b1}(t)}\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Thus, no state variable can become negative. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}_+^7$$\end{document} is positively invariant and all solutions remain nonnegative. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Theorem 3

(Boundedness of solutions) All solutions of system (1)–(7) with nonnegative initial data remain bounded for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}.

Proof

From Lemma 1,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0\le S_h(t),\,I_h(t)\le N_h. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W(t)=Y(t)+N_b(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dW(t)}{dt} = \tau E(t)\left( 1-\frac{W(t)}{\kappa }\right) -\delta _yY(t)-\delta _bN_b(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W(t)=\kappa$$\end{document}, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dW(t)}{dt}\le 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W(t)\le \kappa$$\end{document} for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}.

Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0\le Y(t),\,S_{b1}(t),\,I_{b1}(t),\,S_{b2}(t)\le \kappa . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}From (1),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{dE(t)}{dt} = \lambda N_b(t)-(\tau +\delta _e)E(t) \le \lambda \kappa -(\tau +\delta _e)E(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}By comparison,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E(t)\le \max \left\{ E(0),\frac{\lambda \kappa }{\tau +\delta _e}\right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Therefore, all state variables are bounded. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Corollary 1

The region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega$$\end{document} is positively invariant for system (1)–(7). Moreover, for any nonnegative initial data, the solution exists globally, remains nonnegative, and is bounded for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}.

Model equilibria and basic reproduction number

In this subsection, we determine the equilibrium points of system (1)–(7) and derive the basic reproduction number using the next generation matrix approach24.

Disease-free equilibrium (DFE)

The disease-free equilibrium corresponds to a steady state of the system in which no infection persists in either the host or vector populations. To determine this equilibrium, we set all time derivatives in system (1)–(7) equal to zero.

Infected compartments. From the infected equations,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0=\beta _v S_{b1}^0\frac{I_h^0 e^{-\delta _b\tau _h}}{N_h}-\delta _b I_{b1}^0, \\ 0=\beta _h S_h^0\frac{I_{b1}^0 e^{-\delta _d\tau _v}}{N_{b1}^0}-\delta _d I_h^0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A biologically meaningful equilibrium solution satisfying these equations is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I_{b1}^0 = 0, \qquad I_h^0 = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which corresponds to the absence of infection in the system.

At equilibrium, the delayed terms reduce to their steady-state values, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_h(t-\tau _h)=I_h^0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{b1}(t-\tau _v)=I_{b1}^0$$\end{document}.

Host population. From (6)–(7), the total host population satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_h^0 + I_h^0 = N_h \quad \Longrightarrow \quad S_h^0 = N_h. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Adult vector populations. Using (3) and (5) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_h^0=0$$\end{document}, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0=\phi \gamma _y Y^0-\delta _b S_{b1}^0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad S_{b1}^0=\frac{\phi \gamma _y}{\delta _b}Y^0, \\ 0=(1-\phi )\gamma _y Y^0-\delta _b S_{b2}^0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad S_{b2}^0=\frac{(1-\phi )\gamma _y}{\delta _b}Y^0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hence, the total adult vector population is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N_b^0=S_{b1}^0+S_{b2}^0=\frac{\gamma _y}{\delta _b}Y^0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Egg compartment. From (1),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0=\lambda N_b^0-(\tau +\delta _e)E^0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad E^0=\frac{\lambda N_b^0}{\tau +\delta _e} =\frac{\lambda \gamma _y}{\delta _b(\tau +\delta _e)}Y^0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Nymph compartment. From (2),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0=\tau E^0\!\left( 1-\frac{Y^0+N_b^0}{\kappa }\right) -(\gamma _y+\delta _y)Y^0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Substituting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_b^0$$\end{document} yields\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y^0= \frac{\kappa \,\delta _b}{\delta _b+\gamma _y} \left[ 1- \frac{(\gamma _y+\delta _y)\delta _b(\tau +\delta _e)}{\tau \lambda \gamma _y} \right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Disease-free equilibrium. Therefore, the disease-free equilibrium is given by9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {E}}_0= \bigl ( E^0,\, Y^0,\, S_{b1}^0,\, 0,\, S_{b2}^0,\, N_h,\, 0 \bigr ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E^0=\frac{\lambda \gamma _y}{\delta _b(\tau +\delta _e)}Y^0,\quad S_{b1}^0=\frac{\phi \gamma _y}{\delta _b}Y^0,\quad S_{b2}^0=\frac{(1-\phi )\gamma _y}{\delta _b}Y^0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The DFE is biologically feasible provided\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\tau \lambda \gamma _y}{(\gamma _y+\delta _y)\delta _b(\tau +\delta _e)}>1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which ensures that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^0>0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^0>0$$\end{document}.

Endemic equilibrium

The endemic equilibrium corresponds to the steady state at which the infection persists in both the vector and host populations, that is,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I_{b1}^*>0, \qquad I_h^*>0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}At equilibrium, all derivatives vanish and the delay terms reduce to constants.

Host population. From Eqs. 6)–(7), the total host population satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_h^*+I_h^*=N_h. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Solving (7) gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta _h S_h^*\frac{I_{b1}^* e^{-\delta _d \tau _v}}{N_{b1}^*} =\delta _d I_h^*. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hence,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { S_h^*=\frac{\delta _d N_{b1}^*}{\beta _h e^{-\delta _d \tau _v}}, \qquad I_h^*=N_h-S_h^* } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Infected adult vectors. From (4),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta _v S_{b1}^*\frac{I_h^* e^{-\delta _b \tau _h}}{N_h} =\delta _b I_{b1}^*, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which yields\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { I_{b1}^* =\frac{\beta _v e^{-\delta _b \tau _h}}{\delta _b N_h} \,S_{b1}^* I_h^* } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Susceptible adult vectors feeding on dogs. From (3),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi \gamma _y Y^* =\beta _v S_{b1}^*\frac{I_h^* e^{-\delta _b \tau _h}}{N_h} +\delta _b S_{b1}^*, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}so that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { S_{b1}^* =\frac{\phi \gamma _y Y^*}{\delta _b+\dfrac{\beta _v I_h^* e^{-\delta _b \tau _h}}{N_h}} } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Adult vectors not feeding on dogs. From (5),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { S_{b2}^* =\frac{(1-\phi )\gamma _y}{\delta _b}\,Y^* } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Total adult vector population.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { N_{b1}^*=S_{b1}^*+I_{b1}^*, \qquad N_b^*=S_{b1}^*+I_{b1}^*+S_{b2}^* } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Egg compartment. From (1),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { E^* =\frac{\lambda N_b^*}{\tau +\delta _e} } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Nymph compartment. From (2),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau E^*\!\left( 1-\frac{Y^*+N_b^*}{\kappa }\right) =(\gamma _y+\delta _y)Y^*, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which determines\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { Y^* =\frac{\kappa \delta _b}{\delta _b+\gamma _y} \left[ 1- \frac{(\gamma _y+\delta _y)\delta _b(\tau +\delta _e)}{\tau \lambda \gamma _y} \right] } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Endemic equilibrium point. Consequently, the endemic equilibrium of the delayed system is10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { {\mathcal {E}}^*= \bigl ( E^*,\, Y^*,\, S_{b1}^*,\, I_{b1}^*,\, S_{b2}^*,\, S_h^*,\, I_h^* \bigr ) } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which exists whenever the basic reproduction number satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {R}}_0>1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Basic reproduction number with DFE

The basic reproduction number, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document}, represents the expected number of secondary infections produced by a single infected individual in a completely susceptible population. It serves as a threshold parameter such that the disease dies out if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0<1$$\end{document} and persists if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0>1$$\end{document}.

Using the next-generation matrix approach24–26 and the infected compartments \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{x}}=(I_{b1},\,I_h)^T$$\end{document}, we have at the disease-free equilibrium\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_h^0 = N_h, \qquad I_h^0 = 0, \qquad S_{b1}^0 = \frac{\phi \gamma _y}{\delta _b} Y^0, \qquad I_{b1}^0 = 0, \qquad N_{b1}^0 = S_{b1}^0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}At the disease-free equilibrium, the system is time-independent; therefore, the delayed terms satisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I_h(t-\tau _h)=I_h, \qquad I_{b1}(t-\tau _v)=I_{b1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}This allows the application of the next-generation matrix method in its standard form.

The next-generation matrix at the DFE is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F= \begin{pmatrix} 0 & \dfrac{\beta _v S_{b1}^0}{N_h} e^{-\delta _b\tau _h} \\ \dfrac{\beta _h S_h^0}{N_{b1}^0} e^{-\delta _d\tau _v} & 0 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad V= \begin{pmatrix} \delta _b & 0 \\ 0 & \delta _d \end{pmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Here, the matrix F represents the rate of appearance of new infections in each compartment, while V represents the transition and removal processes, including natural mortality of infected individuals.

Hence, the next-generation matrix is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} K = FV^{-1} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & \dfrac{\beta _v S_{b1}^0}{\delta _d N_h} e^{-\delta _b\tau _h} \\ \dfrac{\beta _h S_h^0}{\delta _b N_{b1}^0} e^{-\delta _d\tau _v} & 0 \end{pmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The basic reproduction number is the spectral radius of K:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {R}}_0 = \sqrt{ \dfrac{\beta _v S_{b1}^0}{\delta _d N_h} e^{-\delta _b\tau _h} \cdot \dfrac{\beta _h S_h^0}{\delta _b N_{b1}^0} e^{-\delta _d\tau _v} }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Substituting the DFE values:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_h^0 = N_h, \quad S_{b1}^0 = N_{b1}^0 = \frac{\phi \gamma _y}{\delta _b} Y^0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {R}}_0 = \sqrt{ \dfrac{\beta _v \frac{\phi \gamma _y}{\delta _b} Y^0}{\delta _d N_h} e^{-\delta _b\tau _h} \cdot \dfrac{\beta _h N_h}{\delta _b \frac{\phi \gamma _y}{\delta _b} Y^0} e^{-\delta _d\tau _v} } = \sqrt{ \frac{\beta _v \beta _h}{\delta _b \delta _d} e^{-(\delta _b \tau _h + \delta _d \tau _v)} }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Final expression in terms of parameters:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { {\mathcal {R}}_0 = \sqrt{\frac{\beta _v \beta _h}{\delta _b \delta _d} \; e^{-(\delta _b \tau _h + \delta _d \tau _v)}}. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Biological interpretation. The expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document} shows that disease transmission is governed by the interaction between host-to-vector transmission (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _v$$\end{document}) and vector-to-host transmission (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _h$$\end{document}), while the mortality rates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _b$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _d$$\end{document} reduce the ability of infected individuals to contribute to new infections.

The exponential terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-\delta _b\tau _h}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-\delta _d\tau _v}$$\end{document} represent survival probabilities during the delay periods. These terms indicate that only individuals surviving the delay intervals can effectively participate in transmission. Consequently, increasing the delays \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _h$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _v$$\end{document} reduces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document}, highlighting the damping effect of biological delays on disease spread.

Sensitivity analysis of the basic reproduction number

To assess the influence of model parameters on the transmission dynamics27,28, we compute the normalized forward sensitivity index of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document} with respect to a parameter p, defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Upsilon _p^{{\mathcal {R}}_0} = \frac{\partial {\mathcal {R}}_0}{\partial p} \times \frac{p}{{\mathcal {R}}_0}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Using the expression\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {R}}_0 = \sqrt{\frac{\beta _v \beta _h}{\delta _b \delta _d} \; e^{-(\delta _b \tau _h + \delta _d \tau _v)}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}we compute the sensitivity indices for the key epidemiological parameters.

For the transmission parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _v$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _h$$\end{document}, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Upsilon _{\beta _v}^{{\mathcal {R}}_0}=\frac{1}{2}, \qquad \Upsilon _{\beta _h}^{{\mathcal {R}}_0}=\frac{1}{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}This indicates that a proportional increase in either transmission parameter leads to a proportional increase in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document}, highlighting their dominant role in disease spread.

Similarly, for the mortality parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _b$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _d$$\end{document},\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Upsilon _{\delta _b}^{{\mathcal {R}}_0} = -\frac{1}{2}, \qquad \Upsilon _{\delta _d}^{{\mathcal {R}}_0} = -\frac{1}{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The negative sensitivity indices indicate that increasing mortality rates reduces the basic reproduction number, thereby suppressing disease transmission.

For the delay parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _h$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _v$$\end{document}, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Upsilon _{\tau _h}^{{\mathcal {R}}_0} = -\frac{1}{2}\delta _b \tau _h, \qquad \Upsilon _{\tau _v}^{{\mathcal {R}}_0} = -\frac{1}{2}\delta _d \tau _v. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fig. 2Sensitivity indices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document} with respect to key epidemiological and delay parameters, illustrating their relative influence on disease transmission.

Sensitivity indices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document} with respect to key epidemiological and delay parameters, illustrating their relative influence on disease transmission.

These expressions show that the delay parameters have a negative impact on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document}. Biologically, this reflects the fact that longer delays reduce the effective transmission due to the survival factors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-\delta _b \tau _h}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-\delta _d \tau _v}$$\end{document}. Consequently, increasing the delay durations lowers the potential for disease spread.

Figure 2 illustrates the relative influence of the key parameters on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document}.
