Figure 4 illustrates the temporal evolution of the infected dog population under subcritical transmission conditions (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0<1$$\end{document}). The solution decreases monotonically to zero, confirming the stability of the disease-free equilibrium and the eventual elimination of infection, in agreement with the analytical results.

Figure 5 shows the corresponding dynamics of the infected vector population. Although a short initial increase is observed due to interaction with infected hosts, the solution ultimately converges to zero. This behavior further confirms that the infection cannot be sustained when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0<1$$\end{document}.

Figures 6 and 7 depict the infected host and vector populations under conditions close to the transmission threshold. In this case, the decay toward zero is significantly slower, indicating prolonged transient dynamics. This highlights the sensitivity of the system near the critical threshold and suggests that small parameter variations can substantially influence disease outcomes.

Figure 8 examines the effect of varying time step sizes on the numerical solution. The qualitative agreement across different step sizes demonstrates the stability and consistency of the numerical scheme.

Figure 9 presents the evolution of all state variables, which converge to a steady state after an initial transient phase. This confirms the boundedness of solutions and the long-term stability of the system.

Figure 10 compares solutions obtained using different discretization levels. The close agreement between curves indicates high numerical accuracy and consistency of the NSFD schemes.

Figure 11 illustrates the impact of delay parameters on the infected dog population. Increasing delay values leads to a noticeable reduction in infection levels, confirming the theoretical role of delay as a damping mechanism in disease transmission.

Figures 12 and 13 further demonstrate the robustness of the numerical schemes, showing that the solutions remain stable and consistent across varying time step sizes and discretization levels.

Figure 14 shows the effect of varying transmission rates. Higher transmission rates lead to increased infection levels and persistence, while lower rates promote disease elimination. This observation is consistent with the dependence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document} on transmission parameters.

Figure 15 presents the numerical error behavior. The error decreases as the time step is refined, and higher-order schemes exhibit improved accuracy, validating the effectiveness of the proposed methods.

Figure 16 provides a heatmap illustrating the relationship between delay parameters and infection levels. The results show a clear inverse relationship, with larger delays leading to lower infection prevalence, reinforcing the regulatory role of delays in the model.

Overall, the numerical simulations are in strong agreement with the analytical findings. They confirm the threshold behavior governed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document}, demonstrate the significant influence of delay parameters on transient dynamics, and validate the stability and accuracy of the proposed NSFD schemes.

Implications for public health in canine Chagas disease control.

The findings can be quite informative for the purpose of controlling canine Chagas disease. The basic reproduction number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document} helps us appreciate the need for controlling the transmission parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _v$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _h$$\end{document}. The importance of controlling the vector-host contact becomes quite clear for controlling the disease.

The sensitivity analysis demonstrates that the increased mortality rate among the vector and host can effectively lower the basic reproduction number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document}. It is thus evident that such control measures work.

The time delay also becomes very significant for lowering the basic reproduction number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document}. Hence, early detection and treatment of infected dogs and interruption of the development process of the vector are quite important.

Applicability of the results

The qualitative findings from the modeling of the system have widespread applicability to the vector-borne spread of Trypanosoma cruzi. Specifically, the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document}, as well as the significance of transmission, mortality rates, and time lags, are properties that are shared by many host–vector models and offer epidemiological insights.

Nonetheless, their quantitative implications depend on the context. In poorly-developed areas, the abundance of vectors and late diagnosis will result in increased transmission and endemicity, but in areas where there are active control measures, better housing conditions and veterinary practices will decrease transmission and the prevalence of the disease.

External socio-economic and environmental variables might also affect the parameters and intervention success.

Relation to existing literature

The obtained results are in accordance with the existing host-vector epidemic models and the models with delays. Namely, the threshold properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0$$\end{document} are in accordance with the classical results according to which the disease will be eliminated if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0 <1$$\end{document}, whereas its existence is guaranteed if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}_0 >1$$\end{document}.

Inclusion of transmission and mortality rates is similar to that of existing studies which identified those factors as important for disease spread. The presence of time delays allows extending those models and considering biological phenomena, like incubation and vector development.

Thus, the current research is in continuity with existing literature through modeling the problem of canine Chagas disease with delays.

Conclusion

This work provided a comprehensive mathematical model that incorporates discrete time delays to explore the dynamics of transmission of canine Chagas disease. This model describes the interaction between the triatomine vectors and the peridomestic dogs and incorporates other essential biological processes including the development, progression of infection, and transmission of the host-vector interaction. The positivity and boundedness of solutions were determined in the qualitative analysis of the model, making it biologically feasible. The presence of equilibrium conditions and the computation of the basic reproduction number gave a formal system of knowledge of threshold conditions on the persistence and elimination of diseases. Moreover, sensitivity analysis provided the most significant parameters that made the dynamics of transmission influential, which has valuable information on possible control measures. First and second-order nonstandard finite difference schemes were developed and studied so that the correct numerical investigation could be made. These schemes were proven to maintain fundamental qualitative properties of the continuous system, such as stability and positivity and exhibit behavior of strong convergence. The use of real epidemiological data increased the validity of the model, and the graphical simulations were used to give a comprehensive representation of the system dynamics. Specifically, the findings demonstrated that delay parameters are also an important regulatory factor as they minimized the level of infection but the rate of transmission was a strong determinant of the disease persistence and spread. In general, the suggested modeling framework is a powerful and efficient instrument of studying the complicated factors that interact to cause canine Chagas disease. The research results can be used in exploring better the host-vector interactions, and help to devise effective intervention and control measures. Further research can be aimed at generalising the model to incorporate spatial heterogeneity, seasonality, and randomness to have a more realistic reflection of the epidemiology of reality. The limitations of the model. In spite of the advantages provided by the proposed delay-driven host–vector mathematical model, there are some limitations that should be taken into consideration. First of all, there is a number of assumptions that are made in relation to the construction of the model, among which the homogeneous mixing of hosts and vectors should be mentioned. Secondly, the values of parameters that are applied during numerical simulations are chosen based on the existing literature and biologically justified estimates, since accurate epidemiological information is not available in relation to canine Chagas disease. This means that the obtained results are supposed to provide information on the nature of qualitative changes. Thirdly, even though time delays have been included into the model, these are considered as discrete and constant, while a distributed delay could have been a better choice. Lastly, it should be noted that the model has not been calibrated or tested against epidemiological and veterinary data.
